When it does, interactions are recoverable. If not, models can generate plausible responses while missing the interaction. We furthermore find that hallucinations increase as targets become less identifiable, and that successful interaction recovery in output space can fail under nonlinear readouts. Finally, we show that aggregate fidelity metrics can conceal whether models learn and extrapolate interactions.


### [149] [SpectrumQuant: Spectrum Alignment for LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20303)
*Dwaipayan Haldar, Indranil Patra, Azhar Yousuf, Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Orthogonal transforms have emerged as an effective tool for low-bit quantization of large language models (LLMs), as they reshape activation distributions without changing the underlying model function. Existing approaches typically rely either on fixed data-agnostic transforms, such as Hadamard rotations, or on calibration-time optimization of learned rotations. We study rotation selection from first principles and show that maximizing covariance leads to spectral alignment and provides a principled criterion for constructing quantization-friendly orthogonal transforms. Based on this insight, we propose SpectrumQuant, a closed-form rotation method that estimates activation covariance from calibration data and uses it to construct a covariance-aware orthogonal transform for post-training quantization. Our formulation characterizes quantization-friendly rotations as compositions of alignment and mixing components, while recovering Hadamard matrices as a special case. Interestingly, a class of butterfly matrices belongs to this category. In the realm of LLM quantization, SpectrumQuant bridges the gap between data-agnostic structured rotations and learned rotations, providing an effective and efficient data-aware alternative for post-training quantization. Experiments on Llama and Mistral models show that SpectrumQuant is competitive with state-of-the-art rotation strategies while requiring less than one-third of the GPU memory and significantly less time than learned rotations approaches like SpinQuant and DartQuant.


### [150] [Comparative Evaluation of Deep-Learning based DoA Estimators for Real-Time ISAC Beamforming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20326)
*Anjali K.S., Manoj Kumar Panda, Chandramani Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Integrated Sensing and Communication (ISAC) systems rely on accurate Direction-of-Arrival (DoA) estimation for effective beamforming and spectral-efficiency enhancement. While numerous classical and deep-learning-based DoA estimation methods have been reported in the literature, their suitability for real-time ISAC operation remains insufficiently understood because practical deployment requires consideration of both estimation accuracy and computational latency. This paper presents a unified evaluation framework for systematically comparing classical and learning-based DoA estimators within an ISAC beamforming pipeline. Representative classical and learning-based DoA estimators, together with an ensemble architecture, are integrated with Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)-based beamforming and evaluated under identical operating conditions. The impact of DoA estimation accuracy, inference latency, beamforming-weight generation time, and resulting effective spectral efficiency is investigated. Results show that different estimators occupy distinct operating points in the accuracy-latency-performance design space. Although learning-based approaches substantially reduce estimation time compared with MUSIC, the overall processing latency increasingly becomes dominated by the computational cost of SVD-based beamforming. Among the evaluated methods, the Regression Convolutional Neural Network (RCNN) exhibits a favorable balance between estimation accuracy, computational efficiency, and beamforming performance, making it a promising candidate for real-time ISAC deployment. The findings further suggest that future low-latency ISAC systems may benefit from beamforming strategies that reduce or avoid computationally intensive SVD-based beamforming-weight generation.


### [151] [Are We Overthinking One-Step Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20331)
*Woobin Park, Jaeyeon Kim, Yilun Du, Sitan Chen, Bohyung Han*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Diffusion and flow matching are now dominant frameworks for generative modeling in continuous domains, but they typically require multiple network evaluations at inference. This has motivated extensive research on one-step generative modeling primarily built on diffusion and flow, though existing approaches often rely on complex training machinery. In this work, we introduce Priming Models, a simple two-stage framework for training one-step generators. Motivated by the difficulty of distributional training from scratch, Priming Models are first trained on a reconstruction objective, e.g., pixel-space denoising at a fixed noise level, before undergoing feature-space distributional post-training. Our central scientific finding is that this simple reconstruction stage is already enough to entirely alleviate the difficulty of distributional training. Empirically, across class-conditional image generation, text-to-image generation, and world modeling, Priming Models significantly advance the Pareto frontier of training compute vs. generation quality. On ImageNet at $256 \times 256$ resolution, we achieve a state-of-the-art FID of $1.19$ among one-step generators using approximately one-tenth the training compute of prior methods with comparable FID. We open-source our codebase at this https URL .


### [152] [Training Dynamics of Transformers on Unbounded Inputs: Attention Learning Can Lag Output Layer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20337)
*Yaomengxi Han, Debarghya Ghoshdastidar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The success of transformers is attributed to several factors, including their capacity to express formal languages over variable-length inputs, and ability of the attention mechanism to focus on relevant input features. However, there is limited understanding of the training dynamics of shallow transformers, specifically how to decouple the learning of the attention and the output (feedforward) layer. A natural extension of the theory of one-step gradient feature learning in feedforward networks suggests that one could first learn the attention weights with a single gradient step, and then train the output layer. We show that this fails for a shallow transformer trained to recognize a star-free language from unbounded-length input. We prove that the transformer can be trained to recognize the language with a three-phase gradient descent: the output layer is updated first, followed by an attention update, and the output layer is fine-tuned in the third phase. In contrast, the language cannot be learned by training only the attention or output layer, or by a single attention-layer update followed by finetuning the output layer. We empirically show that ``attention learning lagging output layer learning" is beneficial in other language recognition, regression, and in-context learning tasks as well.


### [153] [When Does Task-Aware Imputation Matter? An Analysis of Robustness Under Train-Test Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20342)
*Maria V. Salvador, Ana F. Sequeira, Jaime S. Cardoso*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Missing data is a pervasive challenge in machine learning, often degrading predictive performance and complicating model deployment. Most existing approaches treat imputation as a preprocessing step independent of the downstream task, a separation that becomes problematic under train-test mismatches in missingness mechanisms or rates. We introduce a unified framework that treats missing entries as variables in a task-conditioned optimization problem. We instantiate this framework in two settings: an amortized approach for scenarios with missing data at both training and test time, and a latent optimization approach for settings where only training data is incomplete. The evaluation is conducted on 98 tabular datasets, and robustness under train-test mismatches is assessed on 36 datasets. Results reveal that reconstruction error constitutes a poor proxy for predictive utility (Spearman rho approximately 0.16 to 0.32), and that task-aware imputation becomes critical in scenarios where classical methods fail to recover complete-data performance, achieving win rates of up to 80% in challenging train-test mismatch scenarios.


### [154] [Non-asymptotic Convergence of Average-reward Q-learning with Options](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20347)
*Kintan Saha, Ahana Deb, Anders Jonsson, Debabrota Basu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study the finite-sample convergence of Q-learning algorithms for the average-reward formulation of Reinforcement Learning (RL) with options. While options allow temporarily extended and hierarchical actions, the average-reward formulation incorporates continual tasks beyond discounted and episodic RL. We establish the first $\tilde{O}(1/\text{iterations})$ convergence guarantees, in the mean-square sense, for average-reward option Q-learning algorithms in both the inter- and intra-option settings. First, we prove that both inter- and intra-option Bellman operators satisfy multi-step span contractions under unichain assumptions. Then, we propose synchronous estimators of these multi-step option Bellman operators leading to two variants of option Q-learning. Finally, we extend the stochastic approximation framework to prove convergence rates of these two algorithms under the minimal assumptions used in the literature.


### [155] [NeuroDETR: Multiscale Discrete Spatiotemporal Tokenization for Asynchronous EEG Event Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20349)
*Yaozhi Wen, Kun Zhao, Wei Wei, Haoyang Chen, Kangning Wang, Yi Sun, Shaowei Gu, Dingkun Liu, Shuang Qiu, Huiguang He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Asynchronous EEG event detection supports practical brain-computer interfaces by identifying event classes and temporal boundaries directly from continuous recordings without predefined event boundaries at inference time. The primary challenge lies in extracting event-related patterns embedded in ongoing background activity. To address this, we propose \textbf{NeuroDETR}, a discrete spatiotemporal multiscale framework that combines structured tokenization with event-aware feature refinement. Multiscale temporal codebooks capture brief responses and sustained dynamics, while Spatial-Pyramid VQ hierarchically encodes spatial structure across EEG channels. These complementary representations form a compact event memory for jointly classifying events and estimating their onsets and offsets. Before tokenization, we apply FOCAL, an event-aware sparse attention module that uses predicted event presence to guide temporal feature refinement and spatial aggregation, emphasizing likely event periods while preserving context across the recording. Experiments on eight EEG datasets that span diverse paradigms demonstrate state-of-the-art performance. These findings provide a basis for the development of BCI systems that recognize meaningful neural activity during continuous interaction and monitoring.


### [156] [Beyond Expert Rankings: Contrast Calibration for Mixed-Precision MoE Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20353)
*Renjie Xie, Juncheng Yang, Aoting Hu, Yan Liang, Mingxi Zhang, Zheheng Hong, Wei Xu, Yanzhi Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models activate only a few experts per token, yet all expert weights must be stored. Mixed-precision quantization reduces this storage cost by assigning higher bit-widths to sensitive experts and lower bit-widths to resilient ones, but expert rankings alone do not determine how strongly to weight their errors. To address this gap, we propose NatScale, a contrast-calibrated allocation framework for mixed-precision MoE quantization. It uses a model-specific power transformation to calibrate gradient-based expert weights to an information-theoretic contrast target while preserving rankings. We jointly evaluate each expert's gate, up and down configurations to estimate its output error. We then combine these errors and calibrated weights with an existing prior based on weight spectra, variance and precision decay to select configurations under a storage budget. Across three models, NatScale achieves the lowest native-to-quantized KL divergence and the highest top-token agreement in all nine settings against five allocation baselines. It also yields the lowest perplexity in seven settings. These findings highlight the importance of objective-aware precision allocation.


### [157] [Fractional is Better: Learnable Derivative Orders in Neural Operator Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20363)
*Fares B. Mehouachi, Saif Eddin Jabari*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural operators learn mappings between function spaces, enabling fast surrogate solutions to partial differential equations. Despite remarkable architectural diversity, these methods often share a common input representation: raw coordinate-value pairs $(x, u(x))$. We ask whether inputs aligned with PDE differential structure can improve learning. Through Picard iteration on mild solutions, we show that derivatives of the input appear explicitly in the solution operator, suggesting that providing derivative features should reduce the network's implicit differentiation burden. We prove this intuition: providing derivative features improves approximation bounds from $O(W^{-(s-m)/d})$ to $O(W^{-s/d})$, where $W$ is parameter count, $s$ is input regularity, $m$ is the PDE order, and $d$ is spatial dimension. Our central finding, however, is a surprise: the optimal derivative order $\beta^*$ is strictly less than the PDE order $m$. This gap arises from a bias-variance tradeoff in spectral space that we characterize in closed form. Learning $\beta$ from data achieves automatic spectral regularization. We introduce $\partial$-NO (del-NO), for derivative-augmented neural operators, an architecture-agnostic augmentation that provides learnable fractional derivative features to any neural operator backbone. Across benchmark problems and architectures, $\partial$-NO consistently improves prediction accuracy, with learned orders that reflect a representation of known physics modulated by noise and finite-sample constraints.


### [158] [ReAl: Refine neural operators via aligning dynamical flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20369)
*Jing Xu, Zenglin Xu, Li Yi, Ruoxi Jiang, Yuan Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural partial differential equation (PDE) surrogates trained with mean squared error (MSE) can capture dominant dynamics but often lose high-frequency information and fine-scale structures. Generative models (e.g., Flow Matching) can capture the distribution of fine-scale details, yet stochastic errors may accumulate during autoregressive rollouts and compromise long-term stability. To address these limitations, we introduce Refine and Align(ReAl), a model-agnostic plug-in that augments pretrained PDE surrogates to improve the reconstruction of high-frequency structures associated with complex physical phenomena. In the refinement stage, a conditional flow-matching model learns the residual distribution around the deterministic base prediction, recovering missing fine-scale structures while retaining the dominant dynamics. In the alignment stage, to mitigate the generative discrepancies amplified in the autoregressive rollout, particularly in chaotic systems, we generate multiple trajectories from the same input and reinforce the refiner with trajectory-level rewards, thereby achieving long-horizon dynamical alignment. Experiments across diverse surrogate architectures and PDE tasks demonstrate that ReAl consistently improves high-frequency fidelity, forecasting accuracy, and long-horizon stability.


### [159] [From Human Corrections to Learned Segmentations: A Critical Mathematical Review of Information, Learning, and Human Effort](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20396)
*Jon Sporring*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: When does a human correction become useful learning, and what establishes better segmentation at matched human effort? This critical narrative review follows feedback through its selection, learner update, and evaluation. It compares active learning, machine teaching, interactive segmentation, and sequential predictive evaluation using a common mathematical account of target information, predictive error, response predictability, and human work. An exact Bayesian update converts evaluation-label information into expected log-loss improvement; an excess-risk decomposition explains what an approximate learner can lose. Conditional information decompositions separate the roles of selected locations, labels, and displayed context. Empirical comparisons show why response protocols, update mechanisms, and effort denominators matter, distinguishing current-mask refinement from reusable learning and simulated interaction from human evidence. We identify what logs can reveal, what requires independent evaluation, and what remains model-dependent. The resulting research priorities are to test what selection reveals, whether using it improves assimilation, and whether the resulting interaction policy benefits segmentation at matched effort. These are distinct claims: predicting a teacher's responses need not identify human intentions or establish better learning. No new acquisition algorithm or empirical superiority is claimed.


### [160] [Training Language Models to Fail Gracefully](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20401)
*Daman Arora, Xuecheng Liu, Himanshu Gaurav Singh, Fahim Tajwar, Abitha Thankaraj, Ruslan Salakhutdinov, Haiwen Feng, Yuda Song, Andrea Zanette*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning makes language models better at solving tasks, but the same mechanism also rewards guessing. When success is the only way to earn reward, opting for a safe fallback is always worse than a long-shot attempt. When a wrong action can corrupt a database or ship broken code, that incentive can be costly. Therefore, we propose training models to fail gracefully: to preserve successful behavior whenever possible while learning to execute a safe fallback policy when acting would be unreliable. However, naively using policy gradients with intermediate reward for fallback behavior is brittle due to unfavorable optimization dynamics. We address this challenge with GracefulRL, which reshapes the optimization landscape to promote graceful failure. Importantly, this behavior emerges directly from outcome-level reward signals, without annotations specifying when to fall back or additional inference-time machinery such as a verifier. Across a range of tasks and scales, we train models that largely preserve their task-solving capability while selectively falling back when acting would be unreliable. More broadly, our results show that reinforcement learning can not only incentivize models to succeed, but also to reason about the limits of their capabilities and change course when they determine that success is out of reach.


### [161] [Hyperspherical Contrastive Representations Unlock Planning and Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20409)
*Jonghae Park, Sungil Son, Jonghun Shin, Junseo Lee, H. Jin Kim, Benjamin Eysenbach*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We develop a self-supervised reinforcement learning algorithm that learns to build towers of up to eight cubes from scratch, without prior data, rewards, demonstrations, or pretrained models. Our method, Contrastive Planning via Spherical Linear Interpolation (C-Slerp), uses contrastive representations as reusable building blocks and repeatedly composes them to infer a sequence of intermediate waypoints. Under our hyperspherical parameterization, the learned representations allow intermediate waypoints to be inferred in closed form through spherical linear interpolation, without training a separate high-level policy or performing graph search. Importantly, the representation geometry also tells us how to adjust the number of waypoints based on the difficulty of each goal-reaching problem. Experiments show that our method succeeds on long-horizon navigation and robotic manipulation tasks, including settings where strong baselines fail to achieve a single success. Ablations further show that using a fixed number of waypoints substantially degrades performance, highlighting the importance of adapting the plan to task difficulty.


### [162] [When Does Momentum Help in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20413)
*Kaifei Wang, Zikang Shan, Liwei Wang, Yinyu Ye, Han Zhong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Momentum is a classical tool for accelerating convex optimization and a standard component of modern large language model training. Yet recent empirical work has questioned the usefulness of first-moment momentum in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We study this question through a bandit formulation of RLVR with bias-corrected exponential moving average (EMA) momentum, where the EMA coefficient $\beta$ controls how quickly the weights on past gradients decay. When policy gradients are evaluated exactly, we prove that increasing $\beta$ within a range that prevents overshoot strictly accelerates optimization. When gradients are estimated from sampled responses, a positive expected gain can be obscured by sampling fluctuations. We show that as the learning rate $\eta$ decreases, the expected gain with fixed $\beta$ vanishes faster than these fluctuations, whereas increasing $\beta$ to keep $\eta/(1-\beta)$ within the safe regime preserves a nonvanishing gain that eventually dominates them. Bandit simulations quantitatively validate these predictions. RLVR experiments on Qwen3-4B-Base using SGD with EMA and AdamW support our principle of choosing larger coefficients in practical LLM post-training. On Qwen2.5-32B with AdamW, simply increasing $\beta_1$ improves mean validation accuracy by nearly $2$ percentage points over the conventional choice, demonstrating the principle's effectiveness at a larger model scale.


### [163] [One Size Does Not Fit All: Curvature-Aware Adaptive Perturbation for SAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20415)
*Alessia Rossi, Felipe Campelo, Gabriel L. Oliveira*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Poor generalisation in deep neural networks is frequently associated with convergence to sharp minima. Sharpness-Aware Minimisation (SAM) improves generalisation of deep neural networks by minimising a combination of the training loss and the local sharpness of the loss landscape, biasing convergence towards flat minima. However, SAM relies on a fixed perturbation radius $\rho$ that ignores local curvature, leading to inefficient behaviour: the perturbation may be too small to escape poor basins defined by sharp local minima, or too large to allow convergence to solutions with good loss-sharpness tradeoff. We reinterpret $\rho$ as the intensity of the perturbation signal and propose Curvature-Aware Adaptive Perturbation for SAM (CAP-SAM), a geometry-aware method that modulates the perturbation radius based on a sharpness ratio, enabling a more effective search for robust minima on the loss landscape. We prove that CAP-SAM preserves the same convergence rate as standard SAM in non-convex stochastic settings. Extensive experiments indicate that our approach consistently outperforms SAM and its most prominent variants across diverse network architectures on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet.


### [164] [Tracking a Small Set of Experts in Online Convex Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20416)
*Angelos Assos, Anand Brahmbhatt, Elad Hazan*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: For the fundamental problem of prediction from expert advice, Freund asked whether one could compete with a comparator that switches $k$ times among only $m$ experts. For a discrete set of experts, this problem was resolved by Bousquet and Warmuth (2020), who gave an efficient algorithm with optimal regret matching the information-theoretic limit. We study the analogous problem in online convex optimization, where the action space is continuous. An inefficient algorithm can be derived by discretizing the action space and applying the Bousquet--Warmuth algorithm, giving regret of $\widetilde{O}(md+k)$, while the best known efficient algorithm for this problem has regret $\widetilde{O}(dk)$. We show that this computational inefficiency is inherent, establishing a separation between the information-theoretic and polynomial-time regret achievable for this problem. Specifically, under the hypergraphic planted-clique conjecture, we show that for every polynomial-time algorithm there exist instances for which it incurs at least $(k\sqrt d)^{1-o(1)}$ regret.


### [165] [OrionMoE-v1.0: A Mixture-of-Experts Foundation Model for Tabular In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20418)
*Mohamed Bouadi, Nassim Bouarour, Aditya Tanna, Vinay Kumar Sankarapu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Tabular foundation models enable in-context learning by predicting unseen observations from a labeled support table without task-specific parameter updates. Despite recent advances, most existing approaches rely on dense architectures that apply the same transformations across features and datasets. We introduce OrionMoE-Small, a compact tabular foundation model that combines distribution-aware representations with dataset-conditioned sparse mixture-of-experts computation. Built on a dual-axis Transformer architecture, OrionMoE-Small selectively activates experts along the feature axis, with routing decisions informed by both individual feature representations and the broader dataset context. This design enables adaptive allocation of computation across heterogeneous tabular problems while retaining a shared computational pathway across tasks. OrionMoE-Small is pretrained entirely on synthetic classification tasks generated from a structured causal prior and performs downstream prediction without gradient-based adaptation. Experiments across multiple tabular benchmarks demonstrate competitive performance against substantially larger foundation models, with particularly strong results on small-context and high-dimensional datasets. Further analysis reveals favorable relative performance under class imbalance and highlights the importance of feature composition.


### [166] [Continual Learning for Reliable In-Sequence Molecular Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20426)
*Siddhant Setia, Junichi Suzuki, Tadashi Nakano*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: This paper considers reliable in-sequence molecular communication, where bio-nanomachines exchange molecule-encoded information in a specified order, and investigates a new simulation-based method to incrementally evaluate communication protocols by gradually increasing the complexity of experimental settings. The proposed method, called Affine Memory Replay (AMR), builds a continual learning (CL) model to estimate communication performance between bio-nanomachines. AMR stores past simulation samples and synthesizes additional replay samples through interpolation and extrapolation between past samples. Through incremental performance estimation tasks, it replays both past and synthetic samples to regularize model updates, helping the model adapt to new experimental settings while retaining knowledge from earlier ones. Experimental results show that AMR significantly outperforms existing CL methods in maintaining overall estimation accuracy across sequentially learned estimation tasks. It effectively balances memory stability and learning plasticity under moderate training cost.


### [167] [The Accuracy Well: Nonmonotone Accuracy Dynamics at Vanishing Initialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20439)
*Yaroslav Gusev, Eugene Golikov, Dmitry Yarotsky*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study classification dynamics under small initialization and identify a pronounced non-monotone behavior of accuracy that we call the accuracy well: accuracy rises rapidly early in training, subsequently drops, and only much later recovers. We observe this phenomenon in deep linear networks, fully connected ReLU networks on MNIST, deep convolutional networks on CIFAR-10, and controlled synthetic tasks, across different losses and optimizers and in both training and test accuracy. We propose a three-stage explanation in terms of target-driven organization, a degeneracy bottleneck, and subsequent recruitment and recovery. We develop a rigorous theory of these dynamics for shallow and deep linear networks, showing in particular that high accuracy can emerge while the predictor and the change in loss remain asymptotically vanishing, before continued optimization causes accuracy to deteriorate. For a simple synthetic classification task, we prove an extreme instance in which accuracy asymptotically rises to 100%, collapses to 0, and later recovers permanently to 100%. Our results show that classification accuracy can reveal aspects of learning dynamics that are not captured by the training loss alone.


### [168] [Learning by Examples: Power Grid Steady State Analysis with a Tabular Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20462)
*Olayiwola Arowolo, Oluwadara Adedeji, Peter Palensky, Jochen L. Cremer*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Foundation models have shown strong generalization across scientific domains, but their use in power systems remains largely task-specific. We investigate whether a pretrained tabular foundation model (TFM), TabPFN, can serve as a general-purpose neural solver for power grid steady-state analysis. We evaluate the TFM on three tasks: power flow (PF) prediction, N-k contingency screening (NCS), and voltage stability margin (VSM) prediction. We performed experiments on 118- and 2000-bus systems, comparing the TFM against task-specific neural baselines. Without retraining or fine-tuning, the TFM matches or outperforms the best task-specific model in several settings across the three tasks. For 118-bus PF prediction, a variant of the TFM achieves errors of $1\times 10^{-3}$ pu for voltage magnitudes and $0.30^\circ$ for voltage angles, outperforming all baselines. On unseen N-1 contingency and close-to-infeasible PF cases, the TFM is competitive with the DCPF approximation. For millions of unseen N-2 contingencies, the TFM achieves AUCs of 0.92 and 0.95 for branch and voltage violation screening, respectively. The TFM also attains accurate VSM prediction performance for the 2000-bus system with limited labelled data. The TFM attains strong results across the tested tasks despite pretraining without any power-system-specific dataset and requiring no fine-tuning step. These results offer a glimpse into the potential of pretrained TFMs as a neural solver for a range of power grid analysis tasks.


### [169] [Selective Growing Q-Networks (SGQN): An Analysis of Adaptive Action Grids in Decoupled Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20463)
*Parham Nooranbakht, Seyed Mahdi Basiri Azad, Joschka Bödecker*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Value-based Q-learning for continuous control can discretise the action space, but the resolution of the discretisation trades early exploration against final precision. Growing Q-Networks (GQN) grow the resolution uniformly from coarse to fine. We introduce Selective Growing Q-Networks (SGQN), which keeps decoupled Q-learning and replaces uniform growth with an adaptive action grid: bins are added next to bins with high estimated Q-value, bins with low Q-value are pruned, and actions are chosen epsilon-greedily. On eight DeepMind Control Suite (DMControl) tasks with 2 to 12 action dimensions, SGQN has the highest mean final return among the compared methods on four, is within 1.1% of the best method on three more, and is 3.4% below Bang-Bang MPO on Walker Run, using on average 7.5 of 9 available bins per dimension. We then test whether the adaptive grid explains these results, and find that it does not. Growth activates every candidate bin and behaves like uniform growth, and removing pruning changes the return by at most 1.1%. Two other choices matter: starting from a coarse grid, since starting with all 9 bins produces collapsed seeds, and epsilon-greedy selection, since an annealed softmax lowers the return by 23% to 100%. SGQN is insensitive to its growth and pruning hyperparameters. It also differs from the GQN and DecQN baselines in other respects, notably one gradient update per environment step instead of one per eight, which we did not control for, so we do not attribute its advantage to a single component. We present SGQN as a competitive value-based method and as an analysis of which ingredients of a growing action grid matter.


### [170] [Decoupled Marginal Sharpening for Training-Free Inference-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20483)
*Weixin Wang, Wei Deng, Anderson Schneider, Yuriy Nevmyvaka, Pan Xu, Andrew Bennett*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study how to improve LLM reasoning at inference time without updating model weights or using external reward models. Recent reward-free power-sampling methods define the sequence-level power target $\pi_\alpha(y\mid x)\propto p_0(y\mid x)^\alpha$, where $\alpha>1$, which increases the relative probability of completions that are already likely under the base model. Existing methods use Metropolis--Hastings or Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) to approximately sample from this target. However, two challenges arise at different levels. At the target level, full-sequence sharpening can concentrate probability on a few exact reasoning--answer trajectories, suppressing alternative paths that support the same answer. At the sampler level, finite-particle SMC can suffer from path collapse when resampling repeatedly duplicates a few high-weight prefixes. To address these challenges, we propose Decoupled Marginal Sharpening (DMS), which separately controls how strongly the model favors particular reasoning paths and how strongly it favors final answers after aggregating support across those paths. At the target level, DMS first samples from a sharpened reasoning distribution, then marginalizes the model's answer probabilities over the resulting weighted reasoning population and sharpens the induced answer marginal. Separate exponents allow mild reasoning sharpening to encourage exploration of diverse paths, followed by stronger answer-marginal sharpening to concentrate probability on answers with greater aggregate support across those paths. At the sampler level, we develop Fully Adapted Annealed SMC, which combines an annealed sequence of power targets with locally fully adapted proposals and predictive ancestor resampling. Experiments on MATH500, GPQA-Diamond, HumanEval, and AIME24/25 across three base models show that DMS achieves the highest mean pass@1 among the compared methods in all tasks.


### [171] [Average-Reward Policy Identification from a Single Continuing Trajectory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20486)
*Tongxi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, an agent must explore an unknown environment to learn how to make good decisions. Unlike methods that can freely sample any state--action pair, an agent learning from a single trajectory must spend additional time navigating between states. It is therefore natural to expect that learning from a single trajectory requires more experience. In this paper, we show that this additional cost is not always necessary. For average-reward reinforcement learning, we develop an algorithm that learns where to collect information while ensuring that the required samples can be obtained efficiently. We establish an upper bound on the number of interactions needed to identify a near-optimal policy. Furthermore, we prove a matching information theoretic lower bound, showing that, for a broad class of environments and at sufficiently high accuracy, learning from a single trajectory is no harder in the worst case than learning with unrestricted access to state action samples. Our results reveal that the additional complexity cost of navigation can be avoided by adapting information gathering requirements to how the environment provides information.


### [172] [Hindsight-Privileged RLVR: Single-Rollout Token Credit Without Group Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20497)
*Wei Chen, Yubing Wu, Zhou Xu, Haoyue Zhang, Delu Zeng, Jiahao Wang, Junmei Yang, Pan tiewen, Jingfeng Liu, Zhiheng Zhou, Bo Li, Min Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) trains language models from checked task outcomes, but assigning a terminal reward to individual reasoning tokens or actions remains difficult. Group-relative methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) sample several attempts per prompt, but lose their reward-based learning signal when all attempts receive the same outcome. We propose Hindsight-Privileged RLVR (HP-RLVR), a single-rollout method that turns a completed trajectory's own future into token-level update weights. The generating policy snapshot scores each eligible token under its causal history and under a hindsight context containing the realized future and verifier feedback. A normalized Jensen--Shannon divergence between the two views weights the reward relative to a historical baseline, yielding bounded token credit for both successful and failed attempts, with no reference solution or learned critic. In paired Qwen3-8B runs over a shared $100$-step schedule, HP-RLVR trains $1.21\times$, $1.46\times$, and $2.30\times$ faster than GRPO on mathematics, code generation, and ALFWorld, using one eighth the rollouts per prompt. Accuracy is comparable overall, with HP-RLVR ahead on mathematics at all three sizes and GRPO ahead on code and ALFWorld. In ALFWorld, $43.2\%$ (4B) and $49.0\%$ (8B) of GRPO's gradient-norm records are zero, versus none for HP-RLVR.


### [173] [Graph-Enhanced Disease Trajectory Discovery and Temporal Endpoint Forecasting from Longitudinal Electronic Health Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20502)
*Jin-Jia Zheng, Vincent S. Tseng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) can reveal disease-progression structure, while useful forecasting requires leakage-controlled discovery, censor-aware outcomes, and inspectable trajectory evidence. We developed a graph-enhanced framework in MIMIC-IV v3.1 that splits patients before graph construction, learns train-only consecutive-admission transitions, and freezes endpoint-specific branching-motif policies selected on validation data. The final catalog contained 99,527 motifs, and a time-aware Transformer retrieved at most 24 patient-compatible motifs per endpoint through endpoint-specific cross-attention. Across five training seeds, micro-AUPRC increased from 0.9330 to 0.9357, while event-time MAE decreased from 676.9 to 657.7 days (reduction 19.2 days; 95% paired patient-cluster bootstrap CI, 13.8-24.8). Timing effects were heterogeneous: MAE worsened for events within 730 days but improved by 58.9 days for events occurring after 730 days. In an exploratory internal temporal split, branch conditioning reduced conditional event-time MAE by 26.9 days (95% CI, 23.0-30.8) without evidence of better micro-AUPRC. Corrected survival evaluation over the supported 0-2,555-day interval did not establish macro C-index or integrated Brier score superiority over a matched history-only discrete-hazard model. A directed, degree-matched GNN repair substantially improved unseen-edge discovery, yet replacing GNN-derived scores within fixed motif identities did not improve downstream forecasting. These results position branching motifs as structured trajectory context whose main value is long-range timing support rather than uniformly stronger endpoint discrimination, survival prediction, or causal explanation.


### [174] [A Two-Level Clinical Novelty Prioritization Framework for Disease Trajectories Discovered from Longitudinal Electronic Health Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20512)
*Jin-Jia Zheng, Vincent S. Tseng*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Graph-based analysis of electronic health records (EHRs) yields disease-trajectory catalogs, but structural support does not establish semantic novelty. We present a two-level framework to prioritize 99,527 branching motifs previously discovered by an upstream patient-disjoint, train-only MIMIC-IV v3.1 pipeline and frozen after validation-based catalog selection. The framework separates path-level and node-to-endpoint semantic sparsity from reliability-adjusted clinical priority. Three deterministic textualizations per motif or node-target pair were embedded with BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5 and calibrated against 166,972 pre-2023 PubMed references using mean top-20 cosine distance. Positive recombination occurred in 8.63% of motifs and had negligible effect on global ranking, whereas specificity and support/GNN reliability terms changed clinical-priority ordering. Robustness analyses across neighbor counts, templates, encoders, generic-label taxonomies, score settings, PrimeKG, and corrected density-based RND showed that broad ordering was more stable than hard top-k membership. A semantic-dose analysis did not support a monotonic relation between score and forecasting benefit after endpoint and token-count adjustment (1.96 days per SD; 95% CI, -0.75 to 4.68). In the frozen catalog-q90 high-semantic subgroup, branch-enhanced forecasting reduced conditional event-time MAE by 20.1 days (95% CI, 13.3-27.0) and sMAPE by 2.37 percentage points (95% CI, 1.81-2.93), while discrimination and endpoint-ranking differences were inconclusive. Corrected RND showed low tail agreement with the primary score (Spearman rho = 0.430; q90 Jaccard = 0.125) despite a similar aggregate timing association. These findings support representation-aware, uncertainty-aware literature-relative prioritization of discovered trajectories rather than causal discovery or clinician-adjudicated novelty.


### [175] [Bayesian Learning to Select Fair Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20514)
*Saatvik Kher, Hyungrok Do, Judy Zhong, Padhraic Smyth*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Evaluations of classifiers (AI or human or agents) often assume a batch setting in which every agent produces labels on every example in a test set, which can be expensive or impractical in many contexts. This motivates online policies that adaptively select agents to query as examples arrive and update policies as outcomes are observed. We study the problem of selecting, in an online stream, the agent that best balances (i) predictive performance and (ii) subgroup fairness of a sensitive attribute, and that allows for delayed observations of true class labels. We formulate this as a Bayesian multi-armed bandit problem using Thompson Sampling, explicitly balancing accuracy and fairness. We conduct empirical evaluations on real-world datasets spanning problems involving tabular classifiers, large language models, and human labelers, demonstrating substantial reductions in regret relative to baselines. Our theoretical contributions include guarantees on the performance of the selected agent that incorporate both the accuracy and fairness aspects of the problem.


### [176] [The Physics Is the Network: Fast, Training-Free Neural Emulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20525)
*Luka Todorovic, Kaushal Gianchandani, Sai Ravela*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Neural emulation need not require learning a substitute for known physics. We investigate PolyNets that couple a continuous-time neural representation of polynomial dynamics with a simple recurrent network for discrete-time integration. When the governing coefficients are known, the resulting tensor graph requires no training and preserves the prescribed numerical scheme while enabling efficient hardware execution and parallel simulation. Using the one-dimensional Korteweg-de Vries equation, we compare PolyNet with CPU and GPU finite-difference Runge-Kutta solvers and three trained neural models: a state-map emulator, a physics-informed predictor, and a learned-tendency integrator. Analytical soliton solutions provide a reference for amplitude and phase errors across grid resolutions, 10^6 dispersively scaled time steps, ensembles of up to 10^4 trajectories, and varying initial amplitudes. PolyNet achieves accuracy comparable to the numerical solvers, including at amplitudes outside the learned models' training conditions. It combines this fidelity with high GPU throughput, approximately constant over the tested resolution range, and favorable ensemble scaling.


### [177] [Generative Representation Learning for Scalable Latent Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20533)
*Alan Baade, Kyle Sargent, Vincent Sitzmann, Eric Ryan Chan, Justin Johnson, Li Fei-Fei, Gordon Wetzstein*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce an approach for self-supervised representation learning designed to be used as a tokenizer for generative modeling. Our objective supervises a representation loss by factorizing the pointwise mutual information of the data, which we estimate by taking information differences between conditional predictions from a learned generative model. We justify our algorithm theoretically and empirically, demonstrating that our generative approach to representation learning avoids dimensional collapse and feature suppression in cases where commonly used methods like DINO and LeJEPA fail. Moreover, we show our representation learning technique is scalable; expending more compute leads to predictably better latent tokenization. For the first time, we perform a careful analysis of the combined flop efficiency of tokenizer and generative model and demonstrate that our approach is a compute-optimal improvement over direct diffusion for pixel-space generation, obtaining an FID-50K of 2.18 with JiT-L. Applied to ImageNet and the text-to-image dataset GPIC, we show tokenizer encoder scaling laws for diffusability, where extrapolating our approach achieves representations in 7x fewer FLOPs than DINOv2 on ImageNet and 8x fewer FLOPs than WebSSL on GPIC.


### [178] [Revisiting Simple Baselines for Relational Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20534)
*Viet The Nguyen, Vijay Prakash Dwivedi, Gunther Gust*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Relational deep learning has emerged as a powerful paradigm for learning end-to-end predictive models over relational databases by representing them as temporal heterogeneous graphs. Recent advances based on graph neural networks and Transformers have achieved strong performance across diverse domains. However, the effectiveness of simpler relational baselines remains underexplored, making it difficult to assess how much architectural complexity is needed to achieve strong predictive performance. Motivated by the observation that predictive signals arise from target attributes, relational attributes, and historical labels, we revisit how effectively these signals can be exploited using simple propagation methods. We adapt fixed low-pass feature propagation to relational entity graphs and train an MLP for prediction. The resulting predictions are further refined using Correct-and-Smooth, a graph-based label propagation algorithm that incorporates historical labels over task-specific meta-graphs connecting target entities. Across classification and regression tasks on RelBench, an MLP trained on neighborhood-filtered features outperforms one using only target-entity features on most tasks and recovers a substantial portion of the performance of heterogeneous GNNs. Correct-and-Smooth provides further gains from historical labels, although applying it requires a separately constructed meta-graph because connections among target entities are not directly specified by the relational entity graph. Together, our studies revisit the effectiveness of established propagation methods as simple RDL baselines while identifying canonical meta-graph construction as a key challenge for effective label propagation.


### [179] [Detection of Potato Plant Leaf Diseases using Convolutional Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20544)
*Haimanot Bitew Alemayehu, Simachew Melaku Ayalew, Chilotie Desalew Mengesha, Ayisheshim Almaw Bogale, Anteneh Birhan Yenew, Abrham Tegegne Bogale*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Potato is an important food-security crop in Ethiopia, but its production is affected by foliar diseases, particularly early blight and late blight. This study develops a lightweight convolutional neural network (CNN) to classify potato leaves into three categories: healthy, early blight, and late blight. The proposed model was trained using 2,215 images from the PlantVillage dataset and evaluated on an independent field dataset containing 247 images collected from potato farms in Banja, Awi Zone, Ethiopia. On PlantVillage, the model achieved 99.04% training accuracy, 98.21% validation accuracy, and 98.66% test accuracy. On the field dataset, it achieved 90.28% accuracy. The study also compares the proposed CNN with VGG16 and MobileNetV2 in terms of classification performance and computational efficiency. The proposed model contains 446,787 trainable parameters, occupies approximately 1.70 MB, and requires an average of 5.58 seconds per training epoch in the reported experimental setup, compared with 9.96 seconds for VGG16 and 7.68 seconds for MobileNetV2. The results demonstrate the potential of a compact CNN for potato leaf disease classification and highlight the importance of independent field evaluation, where image conditions are more variable than in controlled benchmark datasets. The proposed approach may support preliminary disease identification for smallholder farmers in Ethiopia and similar agricultural settings.


### [180] [Recovering Reliable Supervision via Critique-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20572)
*Kaixiang Zhao, Tianrun Yu, Shangzhe Li, Yuxiao Yang, Amanda Hughes, Weitong Zhang, Taylor W. Killian*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on its own rollouts with token-level supervision from a teacher, assuming that the teacher can reach a correct answer from any prefix the student writes. We call this property the recoverability of the teacher and show it fails on incorrect rollouts: the teacher follows student errors, although it recognizes them. We propose CriticOPD, using error recognition to restore recoverability. The teacher critiques an incorrect rollout, allowing the student to rewrite its solution following the error. The resulting output, including the critique as privileged information, is scored by the teacher while the student uses the revision without the critique for training. On two Qwen3 students and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, CriticOPD improves OPD, with the largest gains on competition problems.


### [181] [One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576)
*Artin Tajdini, Manpreet Kaur, Peng Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Semantics-preserving perturbations of that interface, such as renaming an action or changing the notation, leave the underlying task unchanged yet make the agent worse at it. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. CIPR penalizes the KL divergence between the action distributions induced under the two interfaces, so the policy behaves consistently across them. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.


### [182] [Learnable Hodge-Laplacian Driven Mixture of Experts for Steady-PDE Surrogates on Coarse Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20585)
*Stavros Nousias, Akis Nousias, Konstantinos Gkrispanis*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Current approaches for steady partial differential equations surrogate models on unstructured meshes apply a single mixing mechanism to the whole solution. This paper proposes an architecture that partitions the solution among vanilla transformer experts, each focused on a different spectral scale. The partition is built from a discrete Hodge Laplacian $L_0=\star_0^{-1} d_0^{\top}\star_1 d_0$, that serves as a fitting layer measuring high-frequency content. A gating mechanism takes the mesh as input and produces the per-vertex weights that recombine the expert outputs. AirFRANS is a steady Reynolds-averaged Navier--Stokes (RANS) airfoil benchmark. We evaluate our approach in a reduced remeshed version of the benchmark with each unstructured mesh counting 9.6K faces and 4.9K vertices. We compare our approach with the Transolver on the same dataset and dataset slit. Our approach reduces relative $L_2$ error by 31\% and interior mean squared error by 47\%.


### [183] [Continuum Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20586)
*Afshin Khadangi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We introduce Continuum Attention (CA), a causal Transformer attention operator that replaces discrete key and value lookup with integration over a continuous, piecewise linear memory curve. Each query induces an exponential relevance density along the curve. Integrating the density and interpolated values yields exact normalized attention from closed form exponential moments, including half token boundary contributions. The gradients follow analytically from moments of order at most two, enabling parallel GPU implementations while retaining quadratic sequence complexity and the standard Transformer decoder. Compared with ordinary softmax and focal attention, CA reduces mean validation negative log likelihood by 0.0200 and 0.0480 nats per byte, respectively, winning all paired comparisons. An additional disjoint validation slice yields a 0.0457 nats per byte advantage over the strongest selected softmax baseline, with a paired 95% confidence interval. These findings motivate continuous integration as a promising attention alternative while leaving larger scale generalization open.


### [184] [Your Transformer Is Secretly Elastic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20588)
*Walter Simoncini, Dheeraj Varghese, Gertjan J. Burghouts, Cees G.M. Snoek*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Scaling up transformers has produced models with broad capabilities, deployed across heterogeneous hardware and fluctuating workloads. Yet, model families offer only a few fixed sizes (e.g., S, B, L), forcing users to commit to a model based on hardware and latency constraints. This rigidity is often suboptimal under varying query loads. We introduce a retraining-free method that elastifies pre-trained transformers into a single model that adapts on-the-fly across a continuum of computational budgets. Our key contribution is a differentiable relaxation of the functional unit-ranking problem: unlike prior work, which optimizes a single importance weight per MLP block or attention head, we rank every individual MLP neuron and attention head jointly, on a common scale, by minimizing the model's loss under soft pruning across many sparsity targets, all at once. Across 27 vision and text transformer encoders ranging from 22M to 8B parameters, our method yields smooth degradation up to 60% sparsity and outperforms one-shot baselines under deep pruning. For example, pruning DINOv3 ViT-H+/16 to 50% sparsity removes 427M parameters while reducing ImageNet-1k linear-probing accuracy by only 0.8%, without the need for weight correction or retraining. At 60% sparsity, a pruned AugReg ViT-B/16 model achieves up to $1.80\times$ higher throughput and $1.26\times$ lower single-query latency on an A100 GPU. Elastic models are produced in minutes, with construction time scaling sub-linearly with parameter count.


<div id='cs.DC'></div>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [185] [Parallel Sparse Outputs for Tensor Compilers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19308)
*Adrian Gushin, Pau Sum, Willow Ahrens*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Sparse tensor compilers are commonly used high-performance tools that optimize tensor algebra programs over notoriously complex sparse data structures. Despite the ever-increasing size and scale of sparse datasets, there exists astonishingly little research on developing a parallel sparse tensor compiler. Existing approaches fail to parallelize or make assumptions about access patterns or output formats that limit generality. This paper presents Birdseed, the first tensor compiler to support all loop orderings over any standard sparse format in parallel. More specifically, Birdseed supports parallel sparse scatters and directly writes into list, bytemap, hash, and dense levels of fibertree sparse formats. Our approach exhibits asymptotic memory and time improvements over all previous work. We additionally demonstrate empirical speedups on challenging kernels to parallelize, such as histogram construction and SpMSpV.


### [186] [Scaling LLM Inference via Parallelism: Throughput-Latency Trade-off](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19405)
*Javad Mirzaei, Jeebak Mitra*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) serving systems must balance latency and throughput while efficiently utilizing distributed GPU resources. This challenge arises because inference consists of two fundamentally different phases: prefill, which determines time-to-first-token (TTFT), and decode, which determines inter-token latency (ITL). The use of tensor parallelism (TP), pipeline parallelism (PP), and hybrid configurations exposes different trade-offs between these latency dimensions, making the optimal parallelism configuration highly dependent on workload characteristics, latency requirements, and context length. Moreover, despite the widespread adoption of distributed inference, there is still limited understanding of how parallelism configurations should be selected under varying workload conditions and service-level objectives (SLO). In this paper, we develop a systematic framework for characterizing LLM inference parallelism via throughput-latency trade-offs, SLO-aware serving, and long-context scalability. We introduce the notion of parallelism operating regions by constructing TTFT-ITL feasible sets and Pareto frontiers across different workload compositions, thereby revealing a systematic transition from TP-dominant to PP-dominant configurations as workloads become increasingly prefill-heavy. We further formulate SLO-aware parallelism selection using goodput, showing that stringent TTFT requirements favor PP-dominated configurations, whereas relaxed latency constraints progressively shift the optimality toward TP-dominated configurations with higher serving capacity. Finally, we demonstrate that increasing context length fundamentally changes the relative advantages of parallelism configurations, with PP-dominant configurations exhibiting more favorable scalability and SLO attainment in the long-context regime.


### [187] [The Cost of Context: Profiling the Energy Footprint of Input Tokens in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19819)
*Boris Ruf, Marcin Detyniecki*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: The rapid adoption of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and long-context LLMs has shifted the computational burden toward the prefill phase---the initial stage where the model processes all input tokens in parallel before generating a response---yet its energy impact remains poorly characterised. We investigate the contradiction between massive hardware-level power spikes and the sub-linear marginal cost of input tokens reported in system-level studies. Through high-resolution telemetry of Llama-3.1 and Qwen-2 models on NVIDIA A100 hardware, we identify the efficiency threshold where $O(n^2)$ attention complexity overcomes GPU parallelisation. Our results quantify how optimised memory orchestration (PagedAttention) delays this scaling bottleneck across three orders of context scaling, yielding up to an 8.9$\times$ energy efficiency advantage at 112,000 tokens compared to standard baselines. Furthermore, we characterise the energy disparity between input and output tokens, demonstrating that optimised serving (vLLM) causes this ratio to narrow (from $\approx$850$\times$ to 750$\times$) as context scales due to high decode efficiency, whereas unoptimised baselines show a much smaller, roughly constant gap ($\approx$150--230$\times$) because their prefill is itself expensive. This work establishes context-scaling laws that differentiate engine-level optimisations from inherent model constraints, providing an empirical framework for sustainable LLM deployment.


### [188] [JointServe: Serving Multi-Model Agents on Memory-Constrained GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19822)
*Daijin Hu*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: LLM agents are evolving from a single model calling tools to planners that orchestrate multiple expert models whose capabilities live in distinct weights. This shift breaks a hidden assumption of today's serving systems: that the working set of models fits in GPU memory. On the memory-constrained GPUs typical of private and edge deployments, naive whole-model swapping serializes weight transfer with computation and, without host-backed KV restoration, discards session state at a switch. We present JointServe, a serving system that treats weight shards and KV blocks as residency objects competing for one unified memory budget. JointServe exploits three structural properties of agent workloads (directed calls, gapped execution, and dual-state residency) to prepare models before they are called: an intent signal parsed from the caller's own output stream triggers prefetch during generation, partial k* residency reduces switching to incremental layers, and a preemptive transfer scheduler protects live-session KV with a guaranteed bandwidth share. A carve-out control step adjusts the weight/KV allocation as workload demands change. Evaluations show that JointServe reduces session completion time by 21.7% over the strongest whole-model swap baseline and stays usable in a crash regime where every whole-model swap baseline fails to finish (a 46-fold gap). On replayed SWE-bench, tau-bench, and OSWorld traces, it cuts session completion time by up to 49% and lifts SLO attainment from 2.8-24.5% to 76.8-100%.


### [189] [TrimSO(2): Fast and Memory-Efficient Training of Equivariant Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20029)
*Chen Wang, Yuanchang Zhou, Guangming Tan, Mingzhen Li, Weile Jia*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Equivariant interatomic potentials enable accurate and efficient simulations of molecules and materials. Among them, models based on SO(2) convolutions reduce the cost of tensor products and achieve state-of-the-art accuracy, but their training remains time-consuming and memory-intensive. Existing training implementations materialize large edge tensors for representation transformations surrounding dense matrix multiplications, increasing memory usage and data movement. We observe that these transformations can be integrated into the model's existing dataflow, avoiding separate intermediate materialization. We introduce TrimSO(2), a fast and memory-efficient algorithm for SO(2)-based equivariant models. It realizes this principle through rotation on gather, recombination with activation, and inverse rotation on reduction, with support for the higher-order differentiation required for training. By avoiding intermediate materialization, TrimSO(2) delivers 2.71-16.73$\times$ operator speedups across forward, backward, and double backward and reduces model-level high-bandwidth memory traffic by 3.81-9.88$\times$. Finally, TrimSO(2) achieves 2.07-2.94$\times$ training speedups and 1.53-3.16$\times$ reductions in peak training memory over PyTorch implementations for official eSEN, UMA, and EquiformerV3 workloads on A100 and H100 GPUs.


### [190] [Understanding NVIDIA Grace-Blackwell Unified Memory Through Placement-Controlled Microbenchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20354)
*Nathan Graddon, Aaron Jarmusch, Sunita Chandrasekaran*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: NVIDIA Grace--Blackwell systems make CPU--GPU memory increasingly unified at the programming level, but unified addressability does not make the memory system uniform. A CUDA managed allocation on GB200 may be served from GPU-local HBM, accessed through host/system memory, demand-migrated, prefetched, reused after GPU access, or evicted and reloaded under capacity pressure. The problem is that these states share a programming interface while exercising different hardware paths and runtime mechanisms, making a single ``Unified Memory bandwidth'' number misleading. This paper presents a placement-controlled microbenchmark study of CUDA Unified Memory on NVIDIA GB200, using GH200 as a Grace--Hopper baseline and GB10/DGX Spark as a validation platform. Our methodology separates local HBM access, host/system-memory access, explicit movement, managed first touch, prefetch, reuse, access-pattern sensitivity, oversubscription, and CPU--GPU handoff, then summarizes the observations with a measurement-calibrated model of compute, access, migration, eviction, synchronization, and handoff costs. On GB200, a 1~GB STREAM-style triad reaches 3963~GB/s from local HBM but 304~GB/s through host-mapped CPU/system memory; for a 256~MB streaming kernel, cold CPU-initialized managed memory reaches 14.1~GB/s, while managed prefetch and reuse reach 4606~GB/s and 4504~GB/s. Below the capacity boundary, prefetched and reused managed memory remain above 4.6~TB/s, but at 125\% of CUDA-visible memory they fall to 968~GB/s and 1214~GB/s, showing that Grace--Blackwell unified memory must be understood through placement, access history, locality, reuse, and capacity pressure.


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [191] [Why Static Skill Inconsistencies Do Not Equate to Runtime Risks: A Behavioral Analysis of LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18927)
*Fengrui Fan, Huahao Wang, Jingyi Fang, Yutao Hu, Yueming Wu, Deqing Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: LLM agent frameworks heavily rely on external skill assets to standardize tool usage, encode operational workflows, and supply executable runtime resources. Despite their widespread adoption, multi-component skill packages frequently suffer from cross-artifact inconsistencies, where documentation, scripts, and configuration files specify conflicting behavioral constraints. Although static auditing techniques can uncover such contractual discrepancies, static defect volume cannot reliably reflect real-world agent performance degradation. This gap leaves practitioners without rigorous evidence to assess the practical risk of flawed agent skills. This paper presents a systematic empirical study to demystify the runtime implications of skill inconsistencies. We conduct large-scale auditing over 575 community and benchmark skills, yielding 1,182 manually validated contract inconsistencies. We further build a controlled evaluation pipeline with 49 executable agent tasks and three representative LLMs across NoSkill, OriginalSkill, and FixedSkill experimental settings. Through fine-grained behavioral analysis of 441 paired execution trajectories, we quantify the actual performance impacts of resolved defects.


### [192] [The Agentic Scientist: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Automated Scientific (Co-)Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18933)
*Robert Tjarko Lange, Luca Grillotti, Yutaro Yamada, Rujikorn Charakorn, David Ha*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Modern coding agents are fundamentally reshaping the role of human input for computational research: they write code, launch in silico experiments, analyze their results, and autonomously operate with ever-increasing time horizons. Despite these developments, fully autonomous research agents suffer from a limited ideation radius and myopic hill-climbing search dynamics. To bridge this gap, we introduce The Agentic Scientist, an end-to-end distributed pipeline for human researchers to conduct computational research alongside modern coding agents. Building on its predecessor, our system decomposes research into task drafting, idea generation, planning, sandboxed experimentation, result aggregation, baseline and ablation construction, post-hoc analysis, and report writing. Importantly, each step in the pipeline is powered by the next generation of coding agents and leverages agentic self-verification. The overall research process is driven by a meta-controller that manages parallel research agents and enforces rubric verification at each stage. This design enables the same orchestration layer to be reused across heterogeneous domains with different execution environments, agentic coding harnesses, and evaluation metrics. Our system can be used across a spectrum of autonomy: either as a co-scientist tool or to perform end-to-end scientific investigation. Within the co-scientist setting, we leveraged a development version of our system to submit a single research paper, which got accepted for publication at ALIFE 2026. We further apply The Agentic Scientist fully autonomously to open problem-solving tasks in machine learning, and demonstrate the system's capability of scientific discovery across different domains of computational research. Our ablations suggest that reliable scientific autonomy depends not only on base model capabilities, but also on the cross-agent orchestration layer.


### [193] [Beyond Equation Search: Conjecture-Guided Discovery with SCOPE and ClosureBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18938)
*Yuxiao Hu, Qian Li, Ding Wang, Siheng Chen, Linfeng Zhang, Jinyue Yan, Yuntian Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Most symbolic regression methods optimize candidate equations, but improving an equation's fit does not establish whether its underlying scientific conjecture should be retained, revised, or rejected. A conjecture may admit multiple mathematical realizations, and similarly accurate equations may imply different explanations. This motivates a shift from refining individual expressions to developing and testing the conjectures that guide them. We introduce SCOPE, a framework that sustains multiple conjecture-driven research trajectories. Multiple scientist agents independently pursue conjectures through equation modeling, question formulation, and active experimentation, then exchange evidence to revise their conjectures and research directions. Their findings are consolidated into a scientific report linking models, conclusions, and evidence. Existing equation-discovery benchmarks primarily assess recovered expressions, leaving the evidence and scientific conclusions established through the investigation underexamined. We therefore introduce ClosureBench, an interactive benchmark of 60 closure-discovery tasks across five scientific domains. ClosureBench focuses on closure-discovery tasks to give equation discovery a concrete scientific purpose. Each task embeds an unknown algebraic relation in a known PDE system, where agents control experimental conditions in OpenFOAM to acquire new data for equation discovery. Beyond equation accuracy, ClosureBench proposes three metrics to evaluate the discovery process: evidence faithfulness, mechanism insight, and experimental effectiveness. Compared with diverse baselines, SCOPE achieves higher equation accuracy on both ClosureBench and a real-world scientific discovery case, while producing more accurate, reliable, and effective scientific discoveries.


### [194] [Discovering Undisclosed Concepts in LoRA Adapters for Diffusion-Based AIGC Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18990)
*Yupeng Qiu, Han Fang, Dhananjai Sharma, Ee-Chien Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has made diffusion model personalization affordable and widely shareable, but it also creates moderation risks when harmful or problematic content is not faithfully disclosed. Hence, it is desired to develop a mechanism that, given a LoRA adapter, discovers the concept it was trained to generate, so as to support downstream investigation. We characterize the target concept through the conditioning input that most clearly exposes what the LoRA has learned. Such an input should induce coherent LoRA outputs that are distinctive from those of the base model and strongly align with the learned LoRA subspace. We therefore formulate concept discovery as searching for the conditioning input that jointly maximizes output coherence, LoRA-induced distinctiveness, and subspace alignment. Even with a clear objective, however, optimizing it over an open-ended input space is prohibitively expensive, since reliable evaluation of coherence and distinctiveness requires repeated diffusion rendering. Crucially, we observe that an input need not be rendered to determine whether it is promising. Its alignment with the learned LoRA subspace provides a lightweight signal for localizing promising candidates before visual verification. Based on this insight, we develop a two-stage framework for text-to-image LoRAs. Subspace-Guided Evolutionary Search (SGES) uses render-free subspace alignment to explore candidate prompts and retain a compact shortlist, while Paired Visual Identification (PVI) renders only these candidates and compares the LoRA and base-model outputs to identify those exhibiting coherent and distinctive generation behavior. This design enables efficient and reliable discovery of previously unknown concepts encoded in LoRA adapters.


### [195] [Smart Alone, Blind Together: Why Accuracy Fails to CertifyTheory of Mind in Multi-Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19051)
*Will Beaumaster, Paul Schrater*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Coordinators that act on what they infer about teammates are usually certified by the accuracy of those inferences. We argue that accuracy is the wrong certificate, for two reasons. First, the accuracy needed to act depends on what a wrong action costs, so the bar belongs to the deployment, not the module. Second, a module's errors are rarely independent, and dependence has a sign: positively associated errors help where their damage overlaps and hurt where it compounds, so an accuracy threshold fails in a predictable direction, and once wrong beliefs interact and can do harm, no rule based on accuracy is right for every module. We test these predictions in task assignment, Level-Based Foraging and Hanabi, with injected errors, trained and Bayesian modules and five language models, and in human judgments. Accuracy fails in both directions, approving harmful language-model deployments in Hanabi, where shared errors compound, and rejecting beneficial ones in task assignment, where costly errors are rare. In our tasks it also buys far less redundancy than independence predicts: from GPT-5.4-mini to Claude Opus 5.5, error on teammates the models have evidence about falls from 32% to 11%, but the chance that all three observers misread one falls only from 16% to 9% (independence: 3.4% to 0.15%). We propose certifying the deployment, with a Blind-Spot Gate that prices error costs, pairwise interactions and measured dependence without deploying the module. On held-out data from the cells it was developed on, it makes no significant false approval in 20 splits (accuracy: 3.2 per split), and in preregistered tests on modules it was not developed on, it recovers what accuracy rejects (seven classifiers: regret 0.004 against 0.479) and catches what accuracy approves (a Bayesian module: significant false approvals in 17 of 20 splits for accuracy, 1 for the gate).


### [196] [RLPE: Teaching LLMs to Prompt with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19062)
*Rahul Singhal, Karime Maamari, Pradyumna Tambwekar*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Prompting is a widely used method for adapting general-purpose LLMs to domain-specific tasks. Effective prompts can be tedious to create manually, so prompt writing is increasingly handed to LLMs, with recent work training LLMs to write prompts for other models. These techniques either write prompts without observing how the task model behaves, blind to the failure modes the prompt should address, or must run alongside the task LLM at inference time, adding overhead to every query. Neither reflects how prompt engineering is often practiced. To close this gap, we introduce Reinforcement Learned Prompt Engineering (RLPE), which trains a model to analyze traces of a separate task model and write a prompt targeting the failures it observes. RLPE rewards the trained model strictly on prompt performance, so it learns the prompting strategies that improve the task model. The prompt is generated once and reused across the task, so inference does not require the trained model. Since RLPE needs only query access to the model it prompts, it is also compatible with closed-source models. We apply RLPE to Enigmata, BIG-Bench Extra Hard, and PlanBench. We train a 9B-parameter model that writes better prompts than state-of-the-art frontier models (gpt-5.6-sol and claude-opus-5) and prompt optimizers (GEPA and TextGrad) on 2/3 benchmarks. Although the model is never explicitly trained to iterate on prompts, this capability emerges at a level that matches or beats frontier models. The learned prompting capability also generalizes, with the trained model improving task models 6x larger than the one it trained with, including ones from entirely different model families, and matching claude-opus-5 on a fourth benchmark held out from training.


### [197] [Plan or Retrieve First? A Scale Study of Tool Discovery Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19068)
*Mingfei Zhang, Amit Surana, Ziauddin Majid, Chenxi Wang, Michael Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Tool-augmented LLM agents discover relevant APIs from large registries before planning execution, yet finite context windows prohibit injecting all tool definitions into a single prompt. The dominant approach, which we term Retrieve-then-Plan (RtP), embeds the user query and retrieves similar tools via approximate nearest-neighbor search. We study an alternative Plan-then-Retrieve (PtR) paradigm in which the LLM first generates Abstract Tool Stubs (hypothesized function signatures describing needed capabilities) which then serve as retrieval queries against the registry. Evaluating on ToolBench (46,984 unique API endpoints, 50 categories) across single-tool (G1), intra-category multi-tool (G2), and cross-category multi-tool (G3) query subsets, we find a clear complexity-dependent crossover: RtP outperforms PtR on single-tool queries while PtR outperforms RtP on multi-tool queries.
